EMERGING ECONOMIES

ASIA
China
New monetary policy tool and export controls. The central bank’s Go-
vernor Pan Gongsheng unveiled a plan to switch (“when appropriate”)
its key policy rate from the 7-day reverse repo rate (at 1.4% since May
2025) to the overnight reverse repo rate, in order to reflect develop -
ments in the interbank market. New export controls: The government
plans to add 40 key technologies to the list of technologies prohibited
or restricted for export, including in the AI (for ex. LLMs, computing

clusters), biotechnology and photovoltaic sectors.
Indonesia
BI kept policy rate unchanged at 5.75%, raised its deposit facility rate
by 25bp to 5% and announced a phased cut in reserve requirements
from 9% to 7% by December. This combination of limited FX support
and measures to support credit growth sends mixed signals amidst
pressures on the rupiah following MSCI’s downgrade warning and un-
dermines the central bank’s credibility. The rupiah has lost 1% against

the USD since mid-October, with little contagion effect on other Asian
currencies.
South Korea
The Bank of Korea (BoK) kept its policy rate unchanged at 3.0%, but
the tightening cycle may not be over. CPI inflation was 2.9% y/y in Sep-
tember, above the central bank’s target of 2%, while high oil prices,
rapid growth in Seoul housing prices and wage pressures (though
concentrated in the electronics sector) raise the risk of lasting infla -
tion pressure. As economic activity remains robust, supported by strong

exports and recovering investment, the BoK has scope to raise rates
further.
EMEA
Hungary
Monetary status quo and 2027 budget in consolidation mode. The
Central Bank kept its policy rate unchanged at 5.5%, as expected. A cau-
tious stance will likely prevail in the short term. Inflation has been
picking up in recent months, albeit mildly, and will continue to move
upward as the government recently removed the margin cap on fuel.
Separately, the Budget for 2027 and the medium-term fiscal plan show

Hungary’s goal to join the Eurozone; the government expects to reduce
the fiscal deficit to 4.9% of GDP in 2027 (from 7% expected in 2026) and
towards 2.8% of GDP by 2030.
Türkiye
Cautious monetary loosening. The Central Bank lowered its policy rate
by 100 bp to 36% at its October Monetary Policy Committee meeting,
in line with market expectations. The overnight lending and borrowing
rates were also reduced by 100 bp, to 39% and 34.5%, respectively.

The improvement in underlying inflation has yet to translate into a
sustained period of low inflation, justifying the CBRT’s preference for a
measured pace of monetary easing.
LATIN AMERICA
Brazil
President Lula da Silva was re-elected on Sunday for a fourth term
(with 51.8% of the vote), defeating far right-wing candidate Flavio Bol-
sonaro (48.2%). Lula will enter this new term facing significant econo -
mic and political challenges. Public debt has risen to more than 82% of

GDP , while high borrowing costs and rigid mandatory spending leave
limited room to reconcile fiscal consolidation with social spending and
investment. Restoring confidence in the fiscal outlook will be a key
challenge in the short term.
COMMODITIES
Pressure rising. Amid growing rumours of imminent re-escalation of
military operations in the Gulf, energy prices rose sharply this week,
with brent touching $117/b – near its April peak, and European gas

prices (TTF) rising to 97 €/MWh on Monday, their highest level since
Dec 2022. The Asian gas benchmark (JKM) is following the same trend
(4.6% w/w to 31.4 $/mbtu, equivalent to around 96 €/MWh). Diesel
crack spreads are slightly decreasing in Europe (-2.8% w/w on Monday)
and in the US (-4% w/w on Monday). The first Russian diesel tanker
intended to the US market left Russia this Friday and should supply the
US market in 2 weeks. US diesel average retail prices remain oriented

to the upside (0.7% w/w at 6.32 $/gallon on Monday). US commercial
crude inventories fell by 3.1 mb in the week ending 16 October, signi -
ficantly exceeding market expectations for a 340,000-barrel decline.

Document 335: European resilience, American vigour (2026-10-30)

Report source: BNP Paribas
Date: 2026-10-30
Pages: 2

### Page 1

Paris, 30 October 2026

EcoFlash

European resilience, American vigour

Eurozone growth continues to prove resilient, notwithstanding the volatility of the Irish figures. The Q3 data reinforce 
our forecast of 1.1% growth this year, which could even be slightly exceeded, as German and Spanish momentum shows 
no sign of fading. In France, the negative surprise (-0.1% q/q against a consensus of +0.1% q/q) stems from a new one-
off factor (the sharp fall in aerospace output caused by delays in input deliveries) and masks an encouraging rebound 
in household consumption. In the United  States, growth reached its highest level since Q2 2025 (0.8% q/q), driven by 
buoyant private domestic demand.

Eurozone: The resilience of growth is confirmed 
Eurozone GDP grew by 0.4% q/q in Q3  according to Eurostat's preliminary flash estimate, above both the consensus and our 
Nowcast (0.3%). The ever -volatile Irish figures contributed 0.2 pp to this outcome. Excluding Ireland, growth (0.2% q/q) 
nonetheless remains in line with the pace of recent quarters (0.2 -0.3% q/q on average). This illustrates the Eurozone's capacity 
to withstand the current shocks, thanks in particular to the strength of Spain (+0.07 pp contribution to the q/q change in Eurozone’s 
GDP) and the Netherlands (+0.04 pp), and to Germany's gradual recovery (+0.06 pp). France (-0.03 pp) and Italy (zero growth), 
by contrast, posted disappointing results, although in France's case these partly reflect temporary factors (aerospace output). The 
growth carry-over for 2026 now stands at 1.0%, which supports our forecast of 1.1% in annual average terms and even points to 
upside risk. The European Commission's October surveys remain upbeat, with a further improvement in industrial activity 
expectations. 
The German recovery continues . As anticipated by our  Nowcast, real GDP grew by 0.2% q/q in Q3 (after 0.3% in Q2).  
According to Destatis, consumption (private and public) increased slightly, while investment edged down. Export momentum 
slowed markedly compared with Q2. This is Germany's fourth consecutive quarter of growth. 
Italian GDP surprised on the downside, stagnating in Q3 (against a consensus of 0.1%).  According to Istat, this reflects a 
decline in value added in industry, offset by increases in services and agriculture. The positive contribution from domestic demand 
was reportedly cancelled out by a negative contribution from net trade. 
Spanish growth remained robust in Q3, in line with expectations (0.6% q/q, after 0.7% in Q2),  driven mainly by domestic 
demand (0.5 pp contribution) and, to a lesser extent, by net exports (0.1 pp). Consumption remains the main engine of growth 
(+0.6% q/q for private consumption and +0.9% for public consumption). Investment rose by 0.7%. Export gr owth (1.3%) once 
again outpaced import growth (1.1%) this quarter. All sectors except construction recorded positive changes in value added, with 
industry and services both up 0.7%. 
France: A sharp drop in aerospace output prevents a return to growth in Q3 
GDP contracted by 0.1% q/q in Q3 (after stagnating in Q2), weighed down by a sharp fall in aerospace output (-4.5% q/q 
in the production of other transport equipment, which includes aerospace). This steep decline dragged down goods production 
(down 1.2% q/q) and explains the sharp contraction in exports ( -1.7% q/q) and business investment (-0.4% q/q). Net trade thus 
subtracted 0.6 pp from GDP (imports were flat over the same period). Conversely, household consumption surprised on the 
upside (+0.4% q/q), boosted by purchases of electric vehicles (registrations up 112% y/y in Q3). Public consumption 
(+0.3% q/q) also supported growth, as did activity in market services (+0.3% q/q).  Despite the sharp drop in aerospace 
output in Q3, Airbus has confirmed its delivery targets for the year (with engine supply problems expected to be resolved), pointing 
to a strong rebound in growth in Q4 (0.3% q/q according to our forecasts). Overall, gr owth is expected to reach 0.3% in 2026 , 
before picking up to 0.8% in 2027. 
United States: Growth at a one-year high  
GDP growth picked up in Q3 to 3.4% at a seasonally adjusted annualised rate ( AR; i.e. 0.8% q/q), from 2.2% in Q2 
(0.6% q/q). This is the fastest pace since Q3 2025, confirming that activity is almost immune to the energy shock. Private domestic 
demand remains particularly strong. Private non-residential investment accelerated to 12.7% AR, a performance unmatched since 
the post-Covid reopening. AI-related components are driving this figure (18.5% AR), but other types of investment are also rising 
for a second consecutive quarter (3.8% AR) after contracting uninterruptedly throughout 2025. Household consumption, steady 
at 2.5% AR in real terms, is growing faster than it did before inflation picked up again, but at the cost of a significant drop in the 
saving rate (3.8% at the end of the quarter, down from 4.4% at the end of the previous quarter). The United States nonetheles s 
continues to "import its growth": net trade is weighing on GDP owing to a surge in imports (19.7% AR), driven by AI -related 
components – IT, semiconductors and telecommunications (103.8% AR).

Document 370: LatAm’s silicon dividend_ tailwinds from the AI investment boom in the U.S. (2026-10-28)

Source PDF: all report pdf/LatAm’s silicon dividend_ tailwinds from the AI investment boom in the U.S. (2026-10-28).pdf
Report source: DB Research
Date: 2026-10-28
Pages: 6
Status: ok

### Page 1

Deutsche Bank 
Research Institute

LatAm’s silicon dividend: 
tailwinds from the AI  
investment boom in the U.S.

1 
Authors 
Fancisco Campos 
Chief LatAm Economist

Beatriz Garcia Nunes 
LatAm Economist

October 28, 2026 
Key messages: 
- U.S. investment has broken away from Europe and China since 2023, 
powered by AI-related equipment, software and research spending. 
- The AI CapEx boom demands commodities and merchandise that LatAm 
can supply.  
- Latin America’s export gains are real but concentrated. Copper, computer 
and electronics products provide the clearest links to the U.S. AI buildout. 
- Chile is the region’s most direct copper beneficiary. Mexico captures the 
windfall stemming from increases hardware demand and trade-diversion. In 
these cases, the AI-related tailwinds have undergirded appreciable 
improvements in external accounts. 
- The cases for nickel and zinc illustrate that the dividend is not widespread. 
U.S. imports of the former have mostly returned toward pre-pandemic levels, 
while for the latter bilateral gains partly reflect supplier substitution. 
- While expanding AI use, supply shortages and incentives can extend the 
CapEx boom, its financial viability poses downside risks. 
Another ‘American exceptionalism’ story  
Investment has become one of the sharpest dividing lines among the world’s 
core economic blocs. Since 2024, U.S. gross fixed capital formation has firmed 
up appreciably. Meanwhile, investment in Europe has broadly stalled while in 
China’s it has retreated, the drivers of which are beyond the scope of this report 
(Figure 1).  
The U.S. divergence has largely been an AI story: a burst of spending on 
computing power, digital infrastructure and intellectual property has offset 
weakness in conventional construction and housing. Indeed, total U.S. fixed 
investment is nearly 12% above its 2023 level. Within it, investment in 
information-processing equipment has risen by roughly 57%, software outlays by 
about 35%, and research and development by around 15%. By contrast, non-
residential structures and residential investment sit below their 2023 levels 
(Figure 2).

### Page 2

LatAm Thematic Research

Deutsche Bank Securities Inc. Page 2

Figure 1: The global investment divergence

Figure 2: AI-related categories lead U.S. investment

Source: US Bureau of Economic Analysis, Eurostat, National Bureau of Statistics of 
China, Haver Analytics and Deutsche Bank Research

Source: US Bureau of Economic Analysis, Haver Analytics and Deutsche Bank Research

Latin America’s commodity channel 
AI is not merely a semiconductor story. Chips require specialized minerals; data 
centers require electricity and cooling; electricity requires grids, transformers and 
storage. That chain pulls in copper, aluminum, silver, tin, lithium and selected 
semiconductor inputs, along with natural gas and uranium. Latin America has 
meaningful exposure to several of these inputs, but copper has emerged as the 
commodity with the cleanest and most economically significant regional link. 
Copper is used throughout the AI infrastructure stack, including power cables, 
substations, transformers, server boards, heat sinks and cooling systems. Rising 
U.S. copper demand has lifted imports from Chile and Peru, particularly the 
former (Figure 3). T he effect matters beyond merchandise export volumes. 
Favorable terms-of-trade have helped Chile generate a current -account surplus 
for the first time in roughly fifteen years (0.2% of GDP as of Q2 2026 on a 4 -
quarter rolling basis ) and have allowed Peru to log the largest surplus in about 
forty-five years (4.5% of GDP as of Q2 2026 on a 4-quarter rolling basis).

28 October 2026

### Page 3

LatAm Thematic Research

Deutsche Bank Securities Inc. Page 3

Figure 3: Copper epitomized the commodity link

Source: U.S. Census Bureau, Haver Analytics and Deutsche Bank Research

Not every metal is copper 
The broader mining story demands more caution, though. U.S. nickel imports have 
recovered toward pre-Covid levels, but the upswing has not displayed the same 
persistence as copper. Mexico has captured a disproportionate share of the 
bilateral improvement, yet that gain looks more like normalization than a 
sustained AI -driven break (Figure 4). Zinc tells a similar story. U.S. real imports 
stand shy of their pre-pandemic levels, and stronger purchases from Mexico and 
Peru partly reflect a reallocation away from Brazil rather than a decisive increase 
in U.S. demand (Figure 5).

Figure 4: Nickel: recovery, not a boom

Figure 5: Zinc: a substitution story

Source: U.S. Census Bureau, Haver Analytics and Deutsche Bank Research

Source: U.S. Census Bureau, Haver Analytics and Deutsche Bank Research

28 October 2026

### Page 4

LatAm Thematic Research

Deutsche Bank Securities Inc. Page 4

Mexico’s hardware advantage 
The larger non-commodity prize is in computers and electronics. U.S. imports of 
these products have sky -rocketed, while the supplier mix has shifted away from 
China. Mexico and various Asian economies have gained share on the back of this 
double-whammy impulse (Figure 6). 
This helps explain why Mexico’s non-automotive manufacturing exports, notably 
electronics, have outpaced automotive shipments to the U.S. and pressures from 
higher oil prices. The resulting non -oil trade surplus has offset pressure from the 
oil balance and helped Mexico generate a balanced current account (0% of GDP 
as of Q2 2026 on a 4 -quarter rolling basis). The external boost is meaningful, 
although it sho uld not necessarily be interpreted as bona fide  evidence of 
nearshoring, in our view.

Figure 6: U.S. computer & electronics imports going into overdrive

Source: U.S. Census Bureau, Haver Analytics and Deutsche Bank Research.

How much runway remains? 
Quantifying or mapping out a timeframe for the AI investment outlook is beyond 
the scope of this piece or our expertise. Nevertheless, we’d venture to argue that 
the capex cycle appears more likely to mature gradually than to stop abruptly . 
One reason is that the investment case is shifting from frontier -model training 
toward enterprise inference . Capacity that once served development can 
increasingly run commercial workloads for companies. That creates a path to 
revenue even if artificial general intelligence (AGI) crystallizes later than 
expected. It also spreads demand to a much larger corporate customer base.  
A second reason is scarcity. Graphics processing units (GPUs) remain tight, while 
power availability, grid connections, cooling equipment, construction capacity 
and data -center complexity limit how quickly supply can respond. Bottlenecks 
may therefore exte nd the cycle. The constraint is especially relevant for copper 
28 October 2026

### Page 5

LatAm Thematic Research

Deutsche Bank Securities Inc. Page 5

and power infrastructure, where projects have long lead times and permitting 
hurdles. 
A third reason is strategic. AI development resembles a prisoner’s dilemma at 
both the corporate and national levels. Each participant may worry about costs, 
safety and control, yet slowing unilaterally risks surrendering technological, 
commercial or natio nal security advantages. The same tension is visible in calls 
for guardrails alongside continued spending. Competition can therefore sustain 
capex even when short-term returns are uncertain. 
We acknowledge the risks, nonetheless. Higher borrowing costs raise the hurdle 
rate for an infrastructure buildout financed partly through debt. More important, 
revenues must eventually justify the installed capital base. The bearish case is not 
that AI la cks value; it is that infrastructure spending may run ahead of 
monetization, compromising some companies’ repayment capacity. Public 
opposition to the development of AI infrastructure and/or adoption of AI 
technologies could also slow down the technology’s roll-out. 
The regional balance  
Summing up, the AI investment boom in the U .S. is delivering a dividend to parts 
of Latin America, not the region as a whole. Chile and Peru benefit through copper 
prices and export volumes. Mexico benefits through electronics manufacturing 
and the replacement of Chinese supply. For Brazil and Colombia, the AI dividend 
is rather muted. Where materialized, furthermore, the dividend supports trade 
and current accounts more clearly rather than improving the domestic 
investment. 
These dynamics should persist while U.S. AI investment remains supply -
constrained and strategically protected. If widespread adoption advances and 
the strategic race intensifies, the impulse can last. If financing costs rise further 
or monetization disappo ints, the cycle may narrow. Either way, copper and 
Mexican electronics remain the region’s most visible gauges of the U.S. AI 
investment appetite.

28 October 2026

The scenario, forecasts and nowcasts of the Economic Research

### Page 2

UNITED STATES 
The US economy is expected to grow above its potential in 2026, with an average annual growth rate of 2. 2%, close to the 2025 rate (2.1%) . 
This apparent resilience to the uncertainty, tariff and energy shocks, together with above-trend productivity growth, masks K-shaped growth, 
driven by investment linked to AI-optimism and consumption by the wealthiest amid a wealth effect (historically high stock market valuations). 
Inflation overshooting is set to continue (3.4% in 2026) at least through 2028, largely because of the rise in oil prices and tariffs – although the 
impact of these latter appears to be less significant than expected. The labour market is showing clear signs of improvement that should bring 
the unemployment rate down toward 4.0%. Given this shift in price and employment risks amid dynamic growth, the FOMC proceeded with a 
first rate hike at September’s meeting and we expect two further 25bp increases in December 2026 and January 2027, bringing the Fed Funds 
target range at 4,25% - 4,50%.

CHINA 
Economic growth slowed to 4.3% y/y in Q2 2026 vs. 5.0% in Q1. It is projected to reach 4.6% in 2026 as a whole, down from 5% in 2025. Growth 
remains characterized by a K-shaped trajectory, and the gap between the strong performance of exports and the fragility of sectors that rely 
on domestic demand  has even widened this year . Domestic demand has weakened significantly in recent months . Retail sales have barely 
increased, the crisis in the property sector has continued and household confidence remains low. Fiscal policy measures aimed at supporting 
economic growth are expected to increase in the second half of the year. CPI inflation has rebounded slightly since last March, mostly due to 
the rise in global energy prices, as well as due to the increase in electronic good prices and anti-involution measures implemented by the 
authorities. Deflationary pressures persist, however, given weakness in domestic demand.

EUROZONE 
Eurozone growth is expected to slow to 1.1% in 2026 (from 1.3% in 2025), before strengthening to 1.5% in 2027, supported by higher defence 
and infrastructure spending as well as the global AI capex cycle. The economy has proven resilient to the energy shock, notab ly thanks to the 
increasingly tangible impact of G erman defence spending on the real economy. Elevated energy prices in the autumn (especially for natural 
gas), combined with resilient growth, are set to accelerate inflation to a peak around the turn of the year. Annual average i nflation is thus 
expected to reach  3.0% in 2026 (from 2.1% in 2025), before easing to 2.8% in 2027. Given persistent inflation and resilient growth, we 
anticipate another 25bp hike from the ECB in December 2026, bringing the deposit rate to 2.75%, followed by an extended pause . That said, 
stronger than expected second-round effects could lead to more tightening than our current baseline.

### Page 3

FRANCE 
GDP growth remained low in Q2 (0% after -0.2% in Q1) as a result of deteriorated agricultural and construction output, as well as higher 
inflation (2.4% in 2026 according to our forecast, after 0.9% in 2025) . In 2026, GDP growth should  reach 0.5% (after 0.9% in 2025), driven 
mainly by exports.

UNITED KINGDOM 
UK GDP growth would ease slightly to 1.2% in 2026 (1.3% in 2025), before edging back to 1.3% in 2027. The economy has so far weathered 
both the energy price shock and domestic political volatility. That said, growth is expected to decelerate in the second half  of the year, while 
inflation is set to remain stubbornly above target (averaging 3.2% in both 2026 and 2027), driven by higher energy prices and mounting second-
round effects. In response, we expect the BoE to deliver a single insurance hike in November 2026, bringing the Bank Rate to 4.00%. The BoE 
is then expected to resume easing in H2 2027, with two cuts taking the Bank Rate to 3.50% by end-year.

JAPAN 
Following 1.2% in 2025, Japan's GDP growth is expected to decelerate to 1.0% in 2026, before rebounding to 1.2% in 2027. Higher inflation 
and production costs associated with the energy shock are expected to weigh on the economy’s overall performance. On the other hand, the 
latter is supported by fiscal policy  and AI-related investment. Inflation has generally overshot the 2% y/y target since 2022. Accordingly, the 
Bank of Japan initiated a cautious process of “adjustment in the degree of monetary accommodation” in 2024, lifting the policy rate to 1.25% 
so far (previously negative) – the highest since 1995. We expect two more hikes by the end of Q1 2027, followed by a return to a more cautious 
pace until the terminal rate of +2.5% is reached in H2 2028. Japan is facing long-term rates pressure, illustrated by historically high 10- and 30-
year yields, probably fueled by the level of public debt and the pace of monetary adjustment.

EXCHANGE RATES 
We expect Fed hikes to temporarily offset structural headwinds and support the dollar. EUR/USD would target 1.14 by end -2026, before the 
dollar's structural downtrend resumes toward 1.20 by end-2027, although political and fiscal risk may weigh on the euro next year. The USD/JPY 
appreciation would continue but at a more moderate pace, with the BoJ still behind the curve despite its hikes. By contrast, GBP/USD would 
see relative support underpinned by a modest fiscal risk premium under the new Labour government, and resilient growth.

Document 434: United States _ A shock-resistant expansion (2026-09-24)

Source PDF: all report pdf/United States _ A shock-resistant expansion (2026-09-24).pdf
Report source: BNP Paribas

A SHOCK-RESISTANT EXPANSION
US growth remains resilient despite successive shocks. According to our forecasts, it is expected to reach 2.2% in 2026 and
2.3% in 2027. Its main driver is still investment in artificial intelligence (AI), which has remained entirely unaffected by the
energy crisis. The labour market has seen a marked improvement in job creation and a fall in the unemployment rate. Core

inflation remains stubbornly high and above the Federal Reserve (Fed)’s target. Against this backdrop, the Fed has begun a
hiking cycle in September, which, according to our scenario, would bring the Fed Funds target to 4.50% (upper limit) by Q1
2027. At the same time, long-term rates are under upward pressure.
1  This includes: energy and communications; data centres (facilities); IT equipment and peripherals; communication equipment (equipment); software; and research and develop-

ment (intellectual-property products).
THE ENERGY SHOCK IS NOT HOLDING BACK GROWTH
Driven by AI-related investment, US growth is holding up despite ri -
sing energy inflation. We forecast GDP growth at 2.2% in 2026 and
2.3% in 2027 on an annual average basis. The AI-related investment
cycle1 (+14.8% y/y in H1 2026) is expected to continue, accompanied by
potential productivity gains. However, other private-investment seg -
ments (including residential property) remain sluggish. In addition, the

rising cost of capital could accentuate its ‘K-shaped’ profile. Household
consumption is expected, on the whole, to benefit from the rebound in
job creation and to remain above 2% y/y through to the end of 2027.
In H1 2026, it held up against resurging inflation due to an average
year-on-year increase of 11.5% in tax refunds and a savings rate stan-
ding at its lowest level since 2022 (2.8% in Q2).
RECORD CAPITAL-RAISING DRIVEN BY AI
This year, AI-related investments are driving massive capital-raising

by companies through loans, bond issues or share placements. This
trend is set to continue given the seemingly colossal scale of the fun -
ding requirements, even as risk premia in the bond market begin to
widen. According to their statements, the five tech giants could invest
nearly USD 900 bn this year and over USD 1,000 bn next year—more
than double the figure for 2025. Spurred on by regulatory easing, the
major US banks have announced that they will mobilise significant re-

sources to support projects considered strategic for the United States
(AI, defence, energy and infrastructure). While funding for the AI boom
appears unaffected by rising long-term interest rates, these latter are
likely to continue to weigh on household demand for mortgages. Since
the start of the conflict in the Middle East, 30-year mortgage rates
have risen by 78 bp (to 6.76% at the start of September). Conversely,
consumer lending remained buoyant in H1 2026 and is supporting

household spending.
TWO-SPEED GROWTH IN INDUSTRY
Industrial production rebounded slightly in 2025 (+1.1%), driven by AI
and aerospace. We forecast an acceleration to +1.5% in 2026 and +2%
in 2027. Investment in AI is bolstering the electronics sector, while the
(civil and military) aerospace sector is rebounding as order backlogs
are cleared and defence requirements rise. Conversely, sectors ex -
posed to rising tariffs (in particular, the steel, aluminium and auto -

motive sectors) are still on the back burner, contrary to the objective
of these measures. This positive momentum in industrial production,
while concentrated, is expected to continue, bolstered by the recove -
ry in business sentiment. The ISM Manufacturing Index has been in
expansionary territory since early 2026, despite ongoing pressures on
prices and supply chains.

LABOUR MARKET: A LOW UNEMPLOYMENT RATE ON A DOWNWARD
TREND
Job creation (non-farm payrolls) has rebounded, averaging +80,000 per
month since the start of the year, compared with +10,000 in 2025. This
growth is no longer limited to the healthcare sector. Excluding health-
care, job creation is up by an average of +38,000 in 2026, compared

with -47,000 in 2025. Manufacturing and construction, two sectors
driven by AI, are contributing to this rebound. At the same time, the
labour force has not grown since January 2025 due to the decline in the
foreign-born workforce (down 4.2% cumulatively over the period). Ex -
cluding the impact of COVID-19, the labour force participation rate is at
its lowest level since the 1970s, standing at around 61.5%. The rebound
in labour demand and persistent pressure on the labour supply have

brought the unemployment rate down to 4.1% in August 2026 (a 0.3 pp
fall since the start of the year). The jobless rate is expected to fall to
3.9% at the end of 2027 (4.2% corresponds to full employment, accor -
ding to the CBO) thanks to a job creation rate of around 40,000–60,000
per month.
INFLATION: STRUCTURALLY ABOVE TARGET
The rise in oil prices has triggered a rebound in CPI inflation. The
contribution from the energy component (+1.2 pp) largely accounts for

the rise in inflation since February. A slowdown was seen (from +4.2%
to +3.4% y/y) between the May peak and August. This is expected to
continue due to base effects on energy prices. We anticipate the trough
to be +1.8% y/y in Q2 2027 before a stabilisation at around +2.3% y/y
later in the year.
Meanwhile, core inflation is expected to remain above the Fed’s 2%
target, and above +2.5% y/y in 2027. This highlights the structural
nature of inflation, beyond short-term fluctuations linked to energy.

Non-housing services are expected to show significant inertia, with in-
flation hovering at around +3% y/y by the end of 2027. The potential for
disinflation also appears limited for housing-related services, as the
rise in rents shows no signs of abating. Finally, inflation on non-energy
goods is slowing as the effects of tariffs fade. However, the AI cycle is
exerting upward pressure through the general rise in the cost of elec -
tronic goods.
FED: GREEN LIGHT FOR RATE HIKES

The FOMC has embarked on a cycle of rate increases. The Fed Funds
target was raised to 4.0% (+25 bp) at the September meeting, and we
anticipate two further rises that would bring the policy rate to 4.5% in
January 2027. These three rises (made possible by reassuring develop-
ments in employment and economic activity) are intended to reverse
the three cuts in 2025 adopted to counter the ‘downside risks’ to the
labour market at that time. As a result, the committee can recalibrate

its policy towards ‘price stability’ and, through its interest rate deci -
sions, reflect the priority given to this objective in its communications.
The risks point towards further monetary tightening: in our baseline
scenario, core inflation would remain sticky and the fall in unemploy -
ment would give the FOMC room for manoeuvre to raise rates further.
PUBLIC FINANCES: UNDERLYING DETERIORATION AND PRESSURE ON
LONG- TERM RATES
According to our estimates, the federal deficit is set to widen by 0.7 pp

to -6.6% of GDP in 2026 (by 0.4 pp to -3.1% for the primary deficit),
weighed down by falling tariff revenue (refunds of tariffs already col -
lected and lower-than-expected receipts). In 2027, the end of this one-
off effect is expected to lead to a marginal improvement to -6.3% (to
-2.8% for the primary deficit). The growing burden of interest payments
(3.5% of GDP in 2026 and 3.6% in 2027, following 3.2% in 2025) and
the conflict in Iran (affecting the military budget) are weighing on the

budget balance and federal-debt dynamics. Federal debt is still on an
upward trend, with no particular change in direction, exceeding 100% of
GDP (101.9%, up 3.3 pp, in 2026, and 103.5% in 2027). It is expected to
reach 110% by the end of the decade against a backdrop of budget de-
ficits of close to 6% of GDP , assuming fiscal policy remains unchanged.
Furthermore, the mid-term elections (which see the entire House of
Representatives and one-third of the Senate up for re-election) could

result in a divided Congress or in both chambers changing hands to
the Democratic Party. Should the former occur, this would increase the
risk of a legislative deadlock, while the presidential veto would limit
the Democrats’ ability to influence fiscal policy in the latter scenario.
Against this backdrop, pressure on the bond market is likely to continue
in the coming months. Treasury issuance is rising, fuelled by increa -
sing spending requirements (in areas such as AI, defence and supply

chains). At the same time, shifts in investor demographics are increa-
sing demand volatility, and issuances by companies in the artificial
intelligence sector are intensifying competition for capital. We antici -
pate a 10-year yield of 4.95% in Q1 2027, before a slight easing brought
about by Fed rate rises, which would lend credibility to the price-sta -
bility objective. However, according to our forecast, 10-year yield is
expected to remain close to a 20-year high, at 4.80% in Q4 2027.

THE US RELIES ON IMPORTS FOR ITS GROWTH
The US trade deficit has recently started to rise again. According to
our forecasts, it is expected to account for the same proportion of GDP
in 2026 as it did in 2024 (4.4%). This trend is driven by imports of
AI-related capital goods, which are set to rise from 1.2% to 2.1% of GDP
between 2022 and 2026. Investment in AI is, in fact, import-intensive
(Chart 2), which dampens its positive impact on growth and highlights

the US’s dependence on foreign supplies in a cycle that it is neverthe-
less spearheading.
The additional tariffs have failed to reverse the trend in the trade de -
ficit. Furthermore, the IEEPA tariffs struck down by the Supreme Court
were replaced, in July 2026, by ‘Section 301’ tariffs (forced labour) ran-
ging from 10% to 12.5%. These tariffs target 60 countries and have
been introduced on top of existing sector-specific tariffs (steel, alumi -

nium and automotive). Following these adjustments, the statutory rate
(measured against the 2024 import structure) remained slightly above
10%. The main risks relate to potential new sector-specific tariffs and
the renegotiation of the USMCA.
Article completed on 17 September 2026

我們深度分析  Marvell 併購  Lumentum 將產⽣的綜效，雙⽅投資⼈
為何都不看好，未來 5 年財務衝擊及 outside the box thinking
ANDREW LU ON GLOBAL SEMI/TECHS
NOV 2 ∙ PAID
READ IN APP

What’s new?
Marvell Technology and Lumentum on Nov 2, 2026 announced that
they have entered into a deﬁnitive agreement for Marvell to acquire
Lumentum in an all-stock transaction. Under the terms of the
agreement, Lumentum stockholders will receive a ﬁxed exchange
ratio of 4.5 shares of Marvell common stock for each share of
Lumentum common stock they own. The transaction has an implied
enterprise value of $115 billion for Lumentum, representing
approximately $1,132 per share of Lumentum common stock and a

premium of approximately 23.9% based on each company’s
unaﬀected closing share price as of October 28, 2026, the last
trading day before market speculation regarding a potential
transaction began. Post-closing, current Marvell stockholders will
own approximately 67% of the combined company on a fully diluted
basis, while Lumentum stockholders will own approximately 33%.
但⾃從併購消息提前傳出來， Marvell 股價連續重挫，公司於 11 ⽉ 2 ⽇  收盤
171.41 美元，⽽  Lumentum 每股股東只能拿到 771.345 美元，這⽐起

Lumentum 收盤價 684.4 美元，溢價⼤幅縮⽔到 12.7% ，也⽐上週討論到的
併購溢價⼤幅縮⽔了 32% ，明顯表⽰ Marvell 及  Lumentum 的投資⼈都不看

### Page 2

好這次併購，到底是綜效不⾜，價格太⾼，未來 5 年財務預測衝擊⼤，還是擔
⼼  Marvell 買  Lumentum 在 AI 基礎建設投資週期頂點？
The synergies
Marvell 的核⼼意圖在於從單純的晶⽚設計商，升級為  AI 資料中⼼光電互連
（ Optical Interconnect ）的垂直整合巨擘：
綜效⼀：取得  InP 雷射製造能⼒，控制  CPO 的關鍵供應瓶頸
這可能是本次併購最有戰略價值的⼀部分。當  AI 叢集算⼒從  800G 邁向
1.6T 、 3.2T 甚⾄到  CPO （共同封裝光學元件）時代，⾼速傳輸的功耗與延遲
瓶頸已轉移⾄光電轉換。 Marvell 握有頂尖的  PAM4 DSP 、 TIA/Driver 及
ASIC 交換晶⽚，但⽋缺關鍵的光源硬體。 Lumentum 則掌握化合物半導體
（ InP 磷化銦⾃有晶圓廠）、連續波（ CW ）⾼功率雷射與  OCS （光電路交換）
技術。合併後能實現從數位、類⽐到光⼦端的全鏈路協同設計（ Co-

design ）。
對  CPO ⽽⾔， ASIC 及交換晶⽚只是其中⼀部分。完整⽅案仍需要：
⾼速電訊號處理與光電轉換。
CW Laser 或外部雷射光源（ ELS ）。
光調變器、接收器與光引擎。
⾼密度封裝、光纖耦合及散熱設計。
付費訂閱戶可分享我們對於四⼤綜效分析，雙⽅投資⼈
為何都不看好，在擔⼼什麼？對 Marvell 2027-2031
年的財務預測衝擊及 outside the box thinking
Subscribed
Marvell 買下  Lumentum ，相當於將原本必須外購的關鍵光源能⼒納⼊同⼀
家公司。這有機會改善設計整合、可靠度、功耗及量產交期，也降低受到外部

### Page 3

雷射供應商產能限制的⾵險。但應注意， CPO 不⼀定要求雷射與交換  ASIC 封
裝在同⼀顆晶⽚上；外置雷射仍然是重要的架構選項。
Source: Andrew Lu on global semis/techs
綜效⼆：從銷售  DSP ，升級到銷售完整  Optical Engine
以傳統  800G/1.6T 光收發器為例， Marvell 主要提供電⼦端  DSP 與相關類
⽐晶⽚，⽽  Lumentum 提供光源、光學器件及部分收發器解決⽅案。合併
後， Marvell 可以直接與客戶共同設計光電互連系統，⽽不僅是提供分離式零
件。這個差異的財務意義，是提⾼每條光連結的可取得價值（ Revenue
Content per Optical Link ），⽽⾮只靠光連結出貨量增⻑。尤其在未來  3.2T
光模組、 NPO 與  CPO 世代，系統設計越緊密，晶⽚、光引擎與雷射之間的聯
合驗證價值就越⾼。不過這並不保證客戶會購買整套⽅案。⼤型雲端業者可能
仍採取多供應商策略，並⾃⾏主導系統設計。

綜效三：正⾯挑戰  Broadcom 的  AI 互連⽣態系
過去  Marvell 與  Broadcom 的競爭，主要集中在  Custom ASIC 、 SerDes 、
DSP 、交換晶⽚及網路互連。併⼊  Lumentum 後，競爭範圍將延伸⾄雷射、
光引擎與  OCS ，這場競爭的核⼼不再只是「誰的  DSP 晶⽚⽐較便宜」，⽽是
誰能⽤較低的  pJ/bit 功耗與更可靠的光學系統，協助客戶建造更⼤規模的  AI
Cluster 。然⽽， Broadcom 仍有交換晶⽚市占、客戶關係與成熟網路軟硬體
⽣態系優勢。收購  Lumentum 不代表  Marvell ⽴即追平  Broadcom 的市場
地位。

### Page 4

Source: Andrew Lu on global semis/techs
綜效四：將雷射供應保障轉變成  Custom ASIC 的競爭籌碼
假設  Google 、 Amazon 或其他  Hyperscaler 未來發出新的  Custom AI
ASIC ，其評估標準可能不再只是算⼒、晶⽚價格與  TSMC 產能，⽽是整套  AI
Rack 的：
1. XPU 運算能⼒。
2. Scale-up / Scale-out 頻寬。
3. 光連結功耗與訊號完整性。
4. 雷射供應保障。
5. 整機櫃部署成本及可靠性。
Marvell 如果能同時提供  ASIC 設計與有保障的光學供應鏈，就可能提⾼其在
客戶系統架構設計中的參與度。
Why both Marvell and Lumentum investors worry?
1. 33% 的股權稀釋
全股票收購⼤幅膨脹股本： 交易完成後， Lumentum 原股東將持有合併
公司約  33% 股權， Marvell 既有股東僅剩約  67% 。

EPS 稀釋陷阱： 根據股權結構倒推， Marvell 現有業務每賺  100 美
元， Lumentum 加上淨綜效必須貢獻⾄少約  49 美元的獲利，才能勉強
打平原有股東的每股盈餘（ EPS ）。若  Lumentum 獲利未達爆發性預
期，既有股東權益將被嚴重攤薄。
2. 在週期⾼點買在「天價估值」
Lumentum 股價在收購前已因  AI 光模組需求⼤漲，交易估值隱含⾼達  35 倍
以上的遠期本益⽐。市場質疑  Marvell 是在  AI 基礎設施週期的估值頂峰，溢
價收購⼀家具有⾼度資本⽀出（ CapEx ）與重資產製造屬性的光學元件廠。
Fabless IC 設計公司併⼊化合物半導體晶圓廠，後續擴產將持續吞噬⾃由現⾦
流（ FCF ）。 Lumentum 的  AI 光通訊業務雖有結構性需求，但仍與超⼤型雲

### Page 5

端客戶  CapEx 、光模組需求及光學庫存循環⾼度相關。合併並不必然提⾼收
⼊來源的分散程度，反⽽⼤幅增加對  AI 基建景氣循環的曝險。
3. 漫⻑的交割期（ 2028 上半年）與反壟斷⾵險
官⽅預計交易要到  2028 年上半年才能完成，橫跨近兩年時間。
中國監管變數： 光學元件與通訊雷射⾼度牽動全球雲端與網通供應鏈，
Lumentum 在中國有廣泛業務與供應鏈佈局，這項超⼤型交易極可能遭
遇中國反壟斷機構（ SAMR ）的漫⻑審查與嚴苛附加條件。兩年內若  AI
資本⽀出週期降溫， Marvell 將承受巨⼤的合約與套牢⾵險。
4. 客戶中⽴性⽡解（ Supplier Neutrality ）與競業威脅
晶⽚客戶轉單⾵險： 原本向  Marvell 採購  DSP 晶⽚的光模組廠商（例
如旭創、新易盛等），或使⽤  Lumentum 雷射的網通巨頭（如  Cisco 、
Broadcom 陣營⽣態），可能因  Marvell 跨⼊整機 / 光模組⾃產⾃銷⽽產
⽣排擠與防備，甚⾄引發客戶轉向中⽴競爭對⼿（如  Coherent ）。

商業合作  vs. 資本併購： 雙⽅原本在  2026 年初就已透過商業聯盟共同
展⽰  OCS 技術，投資⼈質疑為何不能以商業代⼯或授權合作，⽽必須付
出千億美元代價冒險合體。
Financial impacts
我們根據彭博社分析師的預測， Lumentum 不管在營收，⽑利，還是
GAAP/non-GAAP 營業利潤及調整後 EPS 的 5 年（ 2026-2031 ）複合增⻑率
皆明顯優於 Marvell ， GAAP/non-GAAP 的營業利潤率也優於  Marvell ，加上
管理階層給出了每年超過  10 億美元的  Run-rate 成本綜效⽬標，並預期在併
購完成後⾸年（ Year 1 post-close ）即可實現  Non-GAAP EPS 的增厚
（ Accretive ）。但  Lumentum 在營收，⽑利，營業利潤的合併後貢獻⽐例皆
明顯不⾜股權稀釋⽐例的 33% ，這讓 Marvell 合併後的  adj EPS 低於併購前
市場預期 adj EPS 達 5-10 個百分點。外加超過 10 倍股東權益淨值的龐⼤溢價

將逐年攤提， Marvell 合併  Lumentum 之後的 GAAP EPS 也將明顯低於之前
預期。

### Page 6

Source: Andrew Lu on global semis/techs, Bloomberg
Outside the box thinking
第⼀， Marvell 正從  Fabless 模式 , 轉變成帶有重要製造資產的半導體集團
⻑期以來， Marvell 的商業模式主要仰賴外部先進晶圓代⼯及封裝⽣態系。
Lumentum 則有⾃有  InP 及其他化合物半導體製造能⼒。這表⽰合併後公司
將同時擁有⾼階  ASIC 設計與特殊半導體製造資產。好處是能控制稀缺製程產
能；壞處是必須承擔設備投資、產能利⽤率與製造良率⾵險。市場可能必須重
新評估  Marvell 是否仍適合享有純⾼成⻑  Fabless ASIC 公司的估值倍數。
第⼆， CPO 將使  Optical Connectivity 的商業模式變得更接近客製化
ASIC

### Page 7

傳統光模組由多家零件商提供標準化產品；未來部分⾼階  CPO 光引擎可能需
要與交換  ASIC 、 XPU 以及系統架構⾼度共同設計。如果這個⽅向成⽴，競爭
將逐漸從單純⽐價格，轉向：
誰掌握系統架構設計。
誰擁有完整  IP 與可靠度驗證能⼒。
誰能提供有保障的量產產能。
誰能在功耗、頻寬與單位成本之間取得最佳組合。
Marvell 可能希望將過去與  Hyperscaler 合作  Custom ASIC 的經驗，移植
到  Optical Connectivity 業務。但我們認為應區分兩個市場：傳統
Pluggable Optics 仍可能保持多供應商與模組化結構，⽽⾼度客製化  CPO
的整合優勢才可能真正擴⼤。
第三，這筆併購也可能是  Marvell 對光通訊獲利分配的⼀種防禦
當  1.6T 、 3.2T 及  CPO 時代來臨，單⼀光連結的成本與價值分配可能改變。
假如  DSP 成本占⽐下降，⽽雷射、光引擎、先進封裝與光學耦合占⽐提升，
原本⾼度依賴  DSP 的公司即使維持市占率，也不⼀定能取得最⼤經濟價值。

Document 456: Why hasn’t there been a major financial crisis in recent years_ (2026-11-03)

Source PDF: all report pdf/Why hasn’t there been a major financial crisis in recent years_ (2026-11-03).pdf
Report source: DB Research
Date: 2026-11-03
Pages: 19
Status: ok

### Page 1

Deutsche Bank 
Research Institute

Why hasn’t there been a major 
financial crisis in recent years?

1 
Authors 
Henry Allen 
Macro Strategist

Peter Sidorov, CFA 
Senior Economist

Camilla Siazon 
Research Analyst

November 3, 2026 
— The economist Hyman Minsky warned, in essence, that “stability is destabilising” . He 
argued that a lengthy period of financial stability could be problematic, as it induced 
risk-taking and complacency amongst investors, laying the foundation for the next 
crisis. For example, lenders relax their terms and borrowers are more willing to extend 
themselves, as nobody wants to miss out. This dynamic makes economies inherently 
prone to financial panics and boom-bust cycles. 
— But since the 2008 crisis, and the subsequent sovereign crisis in the Eurozone, we 
have yet to see another financial crisis happen on a scale that had a lasting market 
and macroeconomic impact. There have been clear moments of concern, as in early 
2023 with the regional bank turmoil, but nothing that has caused a lasting impact. So 
this led us to ask why. 
— In this paper, we examine why the post-GFC era has seen fewer financial crises. 
Explanations include the reforms made after 2008 to avoid a repeat, the lower 
proportion of financial assets held by the banking system, as well as the speedier 
reaction of policymakers when wobbles have occurred. We then consider a few 
candidates that could cause the next crisis, including AI vulnerabilities, private credit, 
and stretched fiscal positions. 
— Ultimately, financial crises haven’t been abolished, but the reforms of recent years 
have made it harder for shocks to propagate through the traditional banking system. 
Moreover, policymakers have become far more inclined to intervene when initial signs 
of stress have emerged.

### Page 2

3 November 2026

Section 1: What is a financial crisis, and what causes them?
There isn’t a single technical definition of a “financial crisis”, and they come in many
different types. Commonly, we think of banking crises like 2008, but they can also
encompass currency/balance of payments crises, debt crises and other forms too.
Given currency and sovereign crises have been more common in emerging markets
(the UK's ERM crisis in 1992 and the euro crisis being the two most notable DM

occurrences), historical analysis in developed economies has tended to focus
heavily on banking crises. Non-bank funding or market-liquidity crises are often not
categorised separately in the literature, but they have become more common in
recent years. This includes the "dash-for-cash" stress at the onset of the Covid
pandemic in March 2020 and the 2022 LDI crisis in the UK.
But for all the different types of financial crisis, there are common themes between

them. Often, they are accompanied by asset bubbles that burst in a non-linear
fashion. We generally see a severe tightening in credit conditions, which in turn
flows through into a broader economic contraction. Frequently, there are
contagion-like dynamics that can be difficult to stop. And in the modern world, they
usually require policymakers to step in, stabilising the situation with interventions.
At risk of oversimplification, we note four factors that determine the emergence and

the severity of a financial crisis. The first three are well-established in the financial
literature, sometimes pooled together into a build-up and a crisis phase
(Brunnermeier & Oehmke 2013), while the fourth has become increasingly
important over the past 15-20 years:
1.  Underlying imbalances. Bubbles and financial imbalances build during
the run-up phase, leaving accumulated risks that materialise when the
crisis erupts. This is the build-up typically described by the early phases of

Kindleberger's model of financial crises.
2.  Trigger. This is the shock that turns an existing vulnerability into clear
financial stress. These are often very difficult to predict and we mostly leave
these outside the focus of this report.
3.  Amplification and financial system fragility. Various factors determine
whether a shock propagates through the financial system, and the
intensity of that shock. These include leverage, liquidity and maturity

mismatches, interconnectedness, and the concentration of exposures.
Other channels in the financial system can then amplify the shock,
including bank runs, fire sales and margin calls, and negative net worth
effects, sovereign–bank loops etc.
4.  Intervention/policy response. This has been increasingly important in
recent decades as the size and scope of policy response to crises has
increased. The outright size can vary, and intervention can occur across

several dimensions, ranging from direct intervention in financial markets,
to counter-cyclical macroeconomic policy that mitigates the impact on the
real economy.
Clearly, these factors are not independent and the interaction between them is what
determines the severity of a financial crisis.

### Page 3

3 November 2026
Thematic Research
Deutsche Bank AG Page 3
One important aspect is the joint role that underlying imbalances and amplification
play. While theoretical models such as Diamond & Dybvig highlight the potential for
a bank run that triggers a broad crisis, empirical research shows that in practice, it
is exceedingly rare for a bank run to lead to bank failure in the absence of an
underlying solvency issue (Correia, Luck and Verner 2026). In other words, the

presence of amplification channels alone does not lead to a crisis.
Conversely, in the absence of amplification, it is possible to get significant
turbulence in asset prices, but without a financial crisis. For instance, a bear market,
wider credit spreads, or even a recession do not in and of themselves imply there
is a financial crisis. This was seen clearly with the bursting of the dot com bubble,
a severe equity decline but not a financial crisis, though it did cause a recession. At

the same time, in the chart below, we can see several US equity bear markets that
took place outside of recessions. On other occasions, however, a “Minsky
Moment” can then happen, where even a seemingly minor event can trigger a high-
profile incident that cascades across the financial system.
Figure 1: S&P 500 drawdowns over time, with shaded recessions showing select episodes when the index fell over 20%
without coinciding with a recession
Source :Bloomberg Finance LP, NBER, Deutsche Bank Research

Looking back through history, we can see how variation in these factors has led to
different regimes over time in terms of frequency/severity of financial factors.

Section 2. Why have financial crises been more or less likely
in certain periods of history?
1. Pre-WWII: More frequent financial instability, albeit under a very
different system.
Financial instability was a recurrent feature of the pre-WWII era. During the 19th
and early 20th centuries, there were frequent panics that often led to major slumps.
For instance, the Panic of 1873 sparked a period often known as the “Long

Depression”, which extended into the 1890s for many countries.
In part, financial panics occurred more frequently because policy operated under
very different settings. For instance, the Gold Standard as the monetary system
constrained a countercyclical policy response and transmitted deflation
internationally, while falling prices increased real debt burdens. Moreover, the
Federal Reserve wasn’t established until 1913, and the lack of an effective lender of

last resort meant there was a private intervention during the US Panic of 1907. The
first wave of globalisation also brought new vulnerabilities. Between 1870 and
1913, many emerging economies accumulated debts denominated in gold or
foreign currency, leaving them exposed when capital inflows reversed or exchange
rates weakened.
The biggest and most famous crisis occurred in 1929, when the Wall Street Crash
of that year sparked the Great Depression. The length and depth of that crisis helped

to stimulate the study of modern macroeconomics, with huge efforts to prevent a
repeat over the years ahead. Indeed, the failures of the response to the Great
Depression played a large role in the much swifter response to the 2008 financial
crisis, helping to avoid a second Great Depression.
That experience established much of the modern financial safety net that exists to
this day. For instance in the US, the Federal Deposit Insurance Corporation was

established in 1933. The US and many other countries came off the Gold Standard
as well in this period. There were also regulatory reforms, and the Glass-Steagall Act
separated out commercial from investment banking.
2. 1944-1971: The Bretton Woods era coincided with fewer financial
crises
Around the end of WWII, economic policymakers sought to create an international
monetary system for the post-war era. The relative financial calm that followed

reflected a combination of restrictions on international capital movement, tightly
regulated domestic financial systems, and a system of fixed exchange rates that
aimed to provide stability and prevent the competitive devaluations that occurred
in the 1930s. This saw exchange rates pegged against the US Dollar, which in turn
was convertible to gold at $35 per ounce.
At the same time, capital controls helped preserve monetary policy flexibility, while

maintaining the system of fixed exchange rates. These controls had several
downsides but they also insulated countries from “sudden stop” crises that can
occur, when capital rapidly flees a country. Moreover, capital controls prevented
the rapid buildup of cross-border systemic leverage. So it was more difficult to
generate a global financial crisis in the same way.

Alongside capital controls, there were also domestic reforms in the aftermath of the
Great Depression, which sought to avoid a repeat. As mentioned earlier, the US
Glass-Steagall Act of 1933 separated commercial banking from investment

banking. Another example of the era’s regulated financial system in the US was
Regulation Q, which imposed maximum interest rates that commercial banks
could pay on deposits. That meant depositors could not search for yield and cause
a run on deposits as easily. Another rationale was that with institutions not
competing for deposits, it would avoid a compression in the margin between the
rates banks paid on their lending and borrowing, which would disincentivise a push

into riskier investments.
Finally, economic growth was also stronger in this period, with the period between
WWII and the 1973 oil shock often described as the “Golden Age”. This played a role
in avoiding financial crises too, as faster growth helped keep debt default rates
down, avoiding the kind of “trigger” events above that might spark a financial crisis.
Coupled with financial repression, strong growth also helped to bring down the
high post-war public debt burdens.

Over time however, the peg between the dollar and gold found itself under growing
strain. The economist Robert Triffin identified the “Triffin dilemma”. Supplying the
world with dollar reserves resulted in outstanding dollar liabilities around the world
that exceeded the gold reserves in the US, weakening confidence that dollars could
ultimately be redeemed for gold. Conversely, restricting dollar supply to protect
those reserves and preserve convertibility risked depriving the system of the

liquidity needed to support trade and growth.
These tensions intensified in the late 1960s amid expansionary US policies,
including spending on the Vietnam War and Great Society programmes. The
continued drain in US gold reserves eventually led the US to suspend the
convertibility of the US Dollar into gold, effectively ending the Bretton Woods
system. Initially, there were attempts to bring some kind of fixed-exchange rate
system back, but the world of fiat currencies (with no link to a precious metal)

persists to this day.

### Page 6

3 November 2026
Thematic Research
Page 6 Deutsche Bank AG
3. 1971-2008: Financial crises become more frequent after the
breakdown of Bretton Woods to the Global Financial Crisis
After the breakdown of the Bretton Woods system, financial crises became a more
regular occurrence. While the end of the gold convertibility did not by itself trigger
future crises, it opened the door for wider financial liberalisation, with the resulting
rise in capital mobility and looser domestic financial regulation then helping to

facilitate a global build-up of imbalances.
The 1980s and 1990s were a period of frequent crisis events. The transition away
from capital controls meant that capital could flow more freely across borders.
However, this could also cause a “sudden stop” if investors suddenly pulled their
capital out of a country, contributing to bank runs and currency collapses.
Moreover, many emerging markets borrowed in foreign currencies like the US
Dollar, meaning that a depreciation in their own currency would see this USD-

denominated debt surge in local currency terms. This meant it was a particular
problem if US interest rates were increasing, as it became harder for many
emerging markets to repay their debts. This was a contributing factor behind the
Latin American debt crisis in the early 1980s. And the rate hikes also had domestic
ramifications, being a contributing factor behind the Savings and Loan crisis in the
United States.
With capital flowing more freely, that also played a role in the 1994 Mexican Peso

Crisis, and the 1997 Asian Financial Crisis. A sudden reversal in capital flows led to
crisis and ultimately recession in many countries. The following year, the Russian
financial crisis then took place, and fears grew in the United States about the wider
ramifications, as the hedge fund Long-Term Capital Management (LTCM) came
under pressure. Indeed, this period saw 75bps of pre-emptive rate cuts from the
Federal Reserve.
This volatility also saw the Fed proactively seek to step in and smooth out the

economic volatility. This was broadly successful, but in the process also helped
engender complacency and a further buildup of leverage. Indeed, after the dot com
bubble burst, the federal funds rate was cut to just 1% in mid-2003, where it
remained until mid-2004.
This period saw clear optimism that another Great Depression was highly unlikely.
For instance, Nobel Prize-winning economist Robert Lucas argued in his 2003 AEA
Presidential Address that “macroeconomics in this original sense has succeeded:

Its central problem of depression prevention has been solved, for all practical
purposes, and has in fact been solved for many decades.”
However, as we now know, the 2008 financial crisis occurred, driving the biggest
global downturn since the Great Depression of the 1930s. It turned out that Minsky
had been correct, and a lengthy period without a crisis had once again helped to
induce complacency, laying the foundations for a repeat.

Section 3: The Post-2008 world: Why have we not had
another big financial crisis?
Since the Global Financial Crisis of 2008, we have thankfully avoided a major
financial crisis. Despite clear moments of stress, most notably around the collapse
of SVB in March 2023, we have not seen a similar wave of bank collapses on that
scale. There are several components to this.
First, post-GFC deleveraging cleared out risks in the system, with countries and
sectors that had excess indebtedness before the GFC experiencing pronounced

declines in leverage.
Second, there have been reforms explicitly designed to avoid a repeat of 2008.
After the crisis happened, there was a clear recognition that gaps in regulation had
exacerbated the crisis, leading to effects that cascaded across the financial system.
For instance, the Basel III reforms required banks to hold more equity, with a
minimum requirement for Common Equity Tier 1 (CET1) capital. They also
introduced the liquidity coverage ratio, which requires banks to hold enough high-

quality liquid assets to ensure they can cover their net cash outflows under a 30-day
stressed scenario. In addition, the net stable funding ratio was brought in, requiring
banks to have sufficient stable funding that exceeded the required amount of stable
funding for a one-year stressed scenario.
At the national level, further reforms were also introduced. In the US, the Dodd-
Frank Act was introduced, with measures including the Volcker Rule that restricted

banks from engaging in proprietary trading, and required the Federal Reserve to
perform an annual stress test of large institutions. Meanwhile in the European
Union, which also experienced a sovereign debt crisis, reforms included a “Single
Supervisory Mechanism”, whereby banking supervision was brought under the
ECB, ensuring harmonised regulatory standards.

### Page 8

3 November 2026
Thematic Research
Page 8 Deutsche Bank AG
Third, the share of financial assets held by banks has fallen since the 2008 crisis.
This hasn’t eliminated risk, but commercial banks are funded by retail deposits that
can quickly fly. By contrast, non-bank financial institutions (NBFIs), e.g. hedge
funds, are not as prone to these bank run dynamics. For instance, a private credit
closed-ended fund is not one where investors can simply redeem their funds on

demand.
We can see this shift in the data. Looking at the FSB’s group of 21 plus the Eurozone,
we can see that the share of assets held by banks has fallen from a peak of 46% in
2008, to 38% in 2024. By contrast, the share held by NBFIs has risen from 43% to
51%.
Figure 5: Share of Assets of Financial Corporations (FSB 21 + EA Group)

Fourth, the lessons of 2008 have meant that when the financial system has had
wobbles since, policymakers have acted with incredible speed to stop the
turmoil.
This was most obvious when the Covid-19 pandemic began. Whilst a pandemic
was very different to a financial crisis, there was initially huge turmoil in financial
markets. Indeed, the S&P 500 fell by more than a third in just over a month, a decline
at a speed and scale unseen since the Great Depression. Meanwhile, US HY credit

spreads soared to levels last seen around the financial crisis, and at the height of the
turmoil, there was a huge spike in real yields, despite the economy entering a
recession.
Against this backdrop, central banks wasted no time in acting. For instance, the Fed
delivered an emergency 50bp rate cut, followed by another 100bp rate cut.
Moreover, they swiftly resumed Quantitative Easing, and announced the
establishment of multiple new facilities to ensure the smooth functioning of credit

markets. These included a Primary Market Corporate Credit Facility, to support new
bond and loan issuance, along with a Secondary Market Corporate Credit facility,
which offered liquidity for outstanding corporate bonds. Meanwhile, a third facility,
called the Term Asset-Backed Securities Loan Facility, was set up to enable the
issuance of asset-backed securities. Similarly in Europe, the ECB set up a new
Pandemic Emergency Purchase Programme (PEPP), which ultimately had a total

envelope of €1.85tn.
Whilst the pandemic caused a huge economic shock, the swift action meant it did
not translate into a financial one, and the brief tightening in credit conditions was

### Page 9

3 November 2026
Thematic Research
Deutsche Bank AG Page 9
unwound over the coming months.
A similar pattern was seen during the regional banking turmoil of March 2023. This
was a financial panic, which initially began with the collapse of Silicon Valley Bank.
However, this began to spread, and Signature Bank also failed. Then in Europe,
Credit Suisse came under investor scrutiny and saw large deposit outflows, which
culminated in a purchase by UBS that included guarantees from the Swiss

government. This coincided with huge market moves, with the 2yr Treasury yield
posting its biggest daily decline in over 40 years, with a 61bps decline on March 13.
However, policymakers swiftly moved to stop any contagion. The US Treasury,
Federal Reserve and the FDIC released a joint statement stating that all deposits at
SVB would be protected, and that the Federal Reserve would provide additional
funding to ensure banks could meet the needs of depositors. Meanwhile, the Fed’s

balance sheet briefly expanded again, despite the fact it had begun the process of
quantitative tightening. These actions ultimately meant the crisis proved contained
and did not lead to a wave of further bank collapses in the manner of 2008.

Fifth, policymakers have also responded to recent shocks with huge fiscal

transfers, meaning that debt has migrated from private towards sovereign
balance sheets.
Whilst global debt has risen slightly over the last decade, if you dig deeper into the
numbers, that’s entirely driven by a rise in government debt. If we compare the
statistics for Q2 2026, relative to Q2 2016, there’s actually been a decline in debt for
households, financial corporations, and nonfinancial corporations. The only one of
the four segments that’s gone up in the last decade is government debt.

In relative terms, the transformation is even bigger if we look back to 2007, before
the Global Financial Crisis kicked off. Back then, government debt was the smallest
of the four categories, but because of the GFC and the pandemic, it’s now become
the biggest of the four categories.
That in turn has led to separate issues, with government bond yields at multi-year
highs, alongside rising net interest spending. So the fiscal space has become
limited. However, public balance sheets don’t face the same kind of system-wide

run that a bank would.

Overall, the post-2008 experience offers three key forward-looking lessons. First,
stronger bank balance sheets and the shift towards non-bank finance suggest

future stress may originate outside banks. As such, historical analyses centred on
banking crises may provide a less complete guide. Second, policymakers have
become increasingly effective at containing liquidity pressures and amplification.
And third, the shift in debt towards sovereign balance sheets has cushioned private-
sector shocks but narrowed the capacity for future intervention.
Section 4: What are potential candidates for the next crisis?

This section considers some of the current risks in the financial system and whether
they could be sowing the seeds for the next financial crisis. This section is not
designed to be a comprehensive list of financial risks, but rather an assessment of
three important areas where risks have risen in recent years.
1. Could AI spark broader vulnerabilities?
A huge driver of the current equity bull run has been the growth in AI-related stocks.
This report won’t explore the debate as to whether this is a bubble, but examine

whether a correction could eventually propagate into a systemic financial crisis. As
noted earlier, a reversal in the investment cycle need not cause a crisis in itself, and
the bursting of the dot com bubble also did not lead to a broader financial crisis.
Nevertheless, there are issues that could spark vulnerabilities. First, the rapid rise
in AI capex raises questions about whether the investment will pay off. The AI build-
out is on course to exceed the scale of past technological investment cycles, which

have often involved overinvestment even when the technologies ultimately proved
transformative. Second, funding is shifting from predominantly operating cash
flow towards external capital, and rising debt issuance in particular. Third, greater
equity-market concentration increases exposure to a reassessment of technology
valuations. Together, these developments make a correction a clear risk.
To assess the potential for systemic stress, we apply the framework in Greenwood,

Hanson, Shleifer and Sørensen (GHSS). This associates heightened crisis risk with
the combination of a strong run-up in asset prices and excessive credit growth,
estimating a 45% probability of a crisis within 3 years if the two thresholds in Figure
11 are met. On a backward-looking basis as of 2025, an economy-wide assessment
finds that the equity-price increase meets the framework’s “red zone” test, but
business credit growth has been considerably more subdued, given the slowdown

### Page 11

3 November 2026
Thematic Research
Deutsche Bank AG Page 11
in credit following the 2022-23 tightening cycle. That is, going into 2026, the pattern
was one of a displacement of credit from other interest-sensitive sectors towards
AI and technology rather than any broad-based rise in leverage.
Nevertheless, US data does show a clear acceleration in credit trends so far in 2026.
To make a more forward-looking assessment, we incorporate the rise in issuance

in 2026 and indicatively extend it into 2027 using the estimated rise in AI capex
plans, assuming credit growth elsewhere in the US economy remains stable
(footnote: We see two-sided risks to this – we may understate the broader credit
demand in tech adjacent areas; conversely, this may understate the drag of higher
rates on credit demand in non-AI related sectors). We also extend the estimated
2027 trend into 2028, including an assumption that equity prices continue their

trend over the past 12 months, to provide an illustrative scenario over the next two
years. Under these assumptions, we would see a visible rise in aggregate US
business sector leverage, leaving the sector just shy of economy-wide red-zone
levels by 2028. As such, approaching that territory by 2028 is conceivable if the AI
cycle continues to accelerate, both in terms of increased debt borrowing and a
renewed rise in valuations.
While the GHSS framework gives a useful quantification of the potential risks, its

indicators also warrant some qualification, which we judge to come in opposing
directions:
n On the one hand, there is a risk that aggregate credit measures may
understate vulnerabilities when borrowing is highly concentrated. Circular
financing, leases and off-balance-sheet structures complicate the picture,
and while we incorporate the recent rise in payables in our leverage metric
to capture some of this risk, it may well be underestimated. Moreover,

realised interest costs remain below current market borrowing rates after
the lengthy period of ultra-low rates, so leverage trends alone may miss the
pressure from rising debt-service costs.
n On the other hand, the price-based metric used by GHSS to assess excess
asset price growth (the 3-year real rise in equity prices) may overstate
valuation excesses given the strong rise in earnings. Indeed, P/E multiples
have already seen a sizeable correction since late 2025, which was most

pronounced for software stocks, and currently there is little evidence of
overly stretched tech valuations, especially when compared to the dot com
boom.
The risk of stress amplification is also likely to depend on the nature of any AI
correction. A downgrading of the AI capex cycle due to slower growth in AI
demand, financing constraints or supply bottlenecks may result in weaker
valuations, leading to negative wealth effects and slower capital expenditure.

However, while this would create an economic headwind, it need not produce a
systemic crisis. And while parallels to the dot com bubble may well be relevant,
currently there are factors that suggest downside risks would be more moderate.
For instance, operating cash flow still plays an important role in financing the AI
buildout, whilst there has been a limited run-up in borrowing and much less
stretched valuations relative to the late-1990s.
Our assessment therefore remains that an AI correction is a clearer risk than an AI-

led systemic crisis at this point. For these risks to become more material, it would
need leverage to keep building but AI returns to eventually disappoint, raising the
likelihood of credit events and amplifying the investment slowdown through the
real economy. We will focus on this interaction between debt-service pressures and

the role of credit events more in the next section.

2. Risks from higher rates and private credit
In the previous section we noted that modest economy-wide debt growth doesn’t
necessarily imply modest credit risk when debt-service costs are rising. As existing
debt reprices or is refinanced, borrowers can face greater pressure even without
additional borrowing. This is a key feature of the debt cycle, whereby vulnerabilities
accumulate during the expansion, and rising rates (often amid a monetary

tightening due to an overheating economy) can then expose them. These risks are
relevant in the context of the current rate hiking cycle, especially as much of the
catch up in debt service costs is yet to play out (see chart below).
With banking-sector risks having moderated since the GFC, the rapid expansion of
private credit and its opaque structure have understandably drawn attention as a
possible source of the next crisis. Parallels with the subprime credit boom have

been periodically made. However, we think these should not be overstated. Even
though the dollar size of the US private credit market is similar to that of subprime
around 2006-07, the market must be assessed relative to the economy (which has
doubled in nominal terms). Moreover, the slower build-up, restrictions on
redemptions and lower risks of direct wealth effects all suggest much more
moderate amplification risks than in 2008.
There are already signs that the private-credit growth cycle is slowing (see chart)

and our credit strategists have noted warning signs including weaker new inflows
into BDCs, increasing payment-in-kind interest and prospective refinancing
pressures. That said, defaults remain limited thus far so these should not be seen
as evidence of an imminent systemic event.

### Page 14

3 November 2026
Thematic Research
Page 14 Deutsche Bank AG
Whether more systemic risks could emerge will depend on issues of solvency,
opacity and capacity for intervention.
n First, while redemption gates can limit immediate cash outflows and
reduce the need to sell illiquid loans under pressure, they do not restore a
borrower’s ability to service debt or absorb underlying credit losses. This
distinction between liquidity protection and solvency is therefore central to

judging whether a slowing credit cycle remains orderly.
n Opacity remains a concern, both in financing structures and in the
distribution of exposures among end-holders, including insurers and other
non-banks. This can make losses harder to identify and potential
amplification harder to assess.
n Policymakers’ success in containing liquidity pressures may not translate
into an effective response to credit events - liquidity provision alone cannot

repair impaired balance sheets. Extending support to opaque non-bank
exposures raises difficult questions about eligibility, loss allocation and the
threshold for intervention.
Quite aside from private credit, the fact that we are back in a rate hiking cycle from
the major central banks also adds to the current risks. Indeed, higher rates bring
down inflation, in part because they tighten financial conditions. But a
consequence of this is that it often reveals vulnerabilities in the financial system,

and makes a crisis more likely. For instance, it was a contributing factor behind the
2023 regional banking crisis, as banks had growing unrealised losses on their
securities portfolio when rates began to rise again. So even if higher rates don’t
directly cause a crisis, they can often be a key contributor. Indeed, hiking cycles
have regularly coincided with some kind of shock, as the experience of recent
decades demonstrates.

3. Stretched fiscal positions and possible NBFI amplifiers
As discussed earlier, the migration of debt towards sovereign balance sheets has

helped cushion private-sector shocks, but this has come at the cost of higher fiscal
risks. Higher public debt and sovereign yields have limited the room for fiscal
stimulus, while persistent inflation has limited the room for monetary stimulus. This
makes another round of coordinated macroeconomic support harder to deliver.
The issue with high public debt is that it if fiscal sustainability is questioned, that can
raise sovereign borrowing costs, which can amplify existing private-sector

vulnerabilities via a tightening in financial conditions and slower growth.
The financial risk posed by sovereign stress will also depend on the financial
amplification channels. These were clear in the European sovereign crisis with the
bank-sovereign loop, but the changing structure of sovereign markets suggests
that the sovereign–NBFI feedback loop may now warrant increasing attention. This
was visible with the 2022 LDI crisis in the UK, while a more recent concern is the run

up in leveraged hedge fund positions in sovereign bond markets, with hedge funds’
sovereign bond exposures more than doubling since 2022 (see chart below). As
noted also by the IMF, this reflects greater issuance and a rising share of sovereign
debt held by hedge funds.
The lessons from Black Monday, LTCM and the LDI crisis offer reassurance that
intervention can contain market stress originating outside the banking system. But
they are not a guarantee that future liquidity shocks will remain economically

benign. The greater scale and interconnectedness of non-bank finance mean that
we can’t simply rely on historical examples today.
Looking forward, a combination of sovereign repricing, leveraged financial
amplification and a more constrained policy response risks proving harder to
resolve than past episodes.

Conclusion
Whilst a major financial crisis has been avoided since the GFC and the subsequent
sovereign crisis, the problem of financial instability has yet to be permanently

solved. On the one hand, reforms to the financial system and the swift actions of
policymakers have helped to prevent a repeat. But as we know from Hyman
Minsky’s warning, lengthy periods of stability can often lay the foundations for the
next crisis, even if these take place slowly. Indeed, risks often shift rather than
disappear.
Looking forward, there are several vectors of risk worth paying attention to. But
financial crises often start in areas where people are paying little attention, or risks

are assumed to be contained. Indeed, it is the areas where risk is underappreciated
which often prove the most vulnerable.
As such, predicting where the next crisis might arise is inherently difficult. But the
topic is a vital one, given the long-lasting damage that financial crises can cause
when they do occur. Indeed, with the fiscal space for sovereigns increasingly
constrained today, the margin for a future policy error is limited.

• AI概念受挫，資金輪動至其他板塊：民主黨全面掌控國會後，市場擔憂AI產業、大型雲端服務與資料中心的監
管與審查將顯著收緊，使相關類股、日台韓股市均下挫，不過資金輪動到其他產業，支撐美股指數維持穩定。
• 財政紛擾仍存但支出展望中性 ，美10年債利率來到5%以下：市場開始反應財政政策可望趨於中性 ，再加上地
緣政治有望緩和，布蘭特原油跌破100美元/桶，支持美國公債利率持續走低至5%以下，自10月高點以來下滑
近50個基點。殖利率下滑輔以金融市場不確定因素降低支撐股市反彈，同步帶動美國信用債利差收斂，11月以
來美國信用債總報酬緩步走揚。

兩黨對立將放大財政期限影響與紛擾，整體財政支出趨於中性
兩黨對立，財政期限的重要性上升
政策影響
2027年的財政動能預計將大致維持中性
4
• 財政期限的重要性上升，帶來政治紛擾：民主黨可利用具強制性的時間節點，作為政策讓步的槓桿。第一個要
注意的時間點是12/11政府支出授權到期，不過將期限延到2027年2至3月的機率較高(否則政府可能關門)。另
外推論1Q27將臨近債務上限，以目前的財政部存款和非常規措施的使用，最多可支應至2027年8月至10月。
• 財政政策整體趨於中性：分裂政府通常意味著較不具擴張性的財政政策，而眾議院掌握聯邦預算的撥款權，將
可能透過拒絕追加或直接限制戰爭預算的方式來迫使戰事降溫 。然民主黨同時也可能與共和黨協議，以撤銷去
年大而美法案的支出削減措施、恢復健康保險補貼等作為交換條件，讓白宮得以增加國防預算。綜合來看，短
線面臨預算協調的紛擾，美債殖利率波動仍可能偏高，但趨勢上政策制衡效果增強，有利減輕殖利率的壓力。

關稅結構可能改變，資料中心監管程度提高但不影響建置趨勢
民主黨將嘗試撤回部分關稅，但整體稅率變化有限
政策影響
對資料中心監管程度將提高，但非禁止建置
5
• 民主黨將嘗試撤回部分關稅 ，但整體稅率變化可能有限 ：民主黨領袖已表示將嘗試撤銷已實施的部分關稅，但
川普政府仍可利用301條款推動新的關稅，整體有效關稅稅率變化可能有限，但其中可能的重點是降低對加拿大
的關稅。
• 對資料中心監管程度將提高，不影響建置趨勢：傳統共和黨票倉的德州變天，而德州是最大的資料中心建設地
區，新任的民主黨州長可能要求企業自行承擔電力及電網擴建成本 、保障居民用水優先權，並在保護規範明確
之前影響資料中心的建設進度。不過本行認為，除了要求企業揭露資料來源與訓練資訊、建立安全治理架構，
以及落實其他風險管理措施等規定之外，尚無太多州級監管制度被認為具有高度限制性。而在聯邦層級，大規
模的新監管措施即使被國會提出，也很可能被川普否決，且AI競爭力仍是跨黨派國策，建設趨勢將持續推進。

AI 資料中心已從「產業政策」轉變為「民生電價議題」
超大型業者算力供給轉吃緊
產業影響
2030年電力缺口收斂
6
• 德州資料中心暫停令，AI算力布局的轉變與供需影響：德州8月初頒布的暫停令，同步凍結了新資料中心的興建
（僅限未動工新案）與電力輸送。在扣除被淘汰的虛報專案需求後，預估部分專案將外溢至其他電力更吃緊的州，
導致全美電力缺口淨擴大約 3–6 GW。若Hinojosa 就任後延續暫停令執行超過一年，其影響2028 年全美電力缺
口將進一步擴大，2030年供需缺口預估將逐步收斂。另一方面，暫停令讓2028年本就吃緊的算力市場更緊，維
持算力租金的高檔區間，對超大型業者而言建置成本增加，與手上既有的算力可以提高售價，兩者相抵，2028
年企業獲利的預估影響僅在1-3%水準。
• 提高監管是正在發生的實質逆風，喊停是低機率的極端政治口號：AI 技術攸關美國的核心基本國力。民主黨的
政策向來主張「調整與監管」，而非全面「廢止」。因此，未來更嚴格的許可審查與補助限制，勢必會提高雲

端大廠在德州擴張的時間成本與資本風險。然而，也可能推動針對資料中心的特定稅種，例如聯邦總收入稅，
或針對資料中心電力消耗的消費稅。民主黨也有可能在新會期提出比共和黨更強制性更嚴格監管的版本 。民主
黨掌控國會後，最可能成法的是「電費成本分攤」立法，因為川普已將此議題列為自己的政績。

健護最具兩黨協商的價值，醫療產業相對受惠
12/11為醫療補貼可以放進談判的時間點
產業影響
健護未來一年的本益比仍合理
7
• ACA補貼及醫療補助是最可能與川普達成交易的領域 ：民主黨贏得參眾兩院，不等於民主黨執政，兩黨仍須協
商。就健護產業而言，相對有利影響的領域在2025年底已到期的<平價醫療法案(ACA)>增強型保費補貼，受淨保
費上升，已有超過200萬人放棄投保 ，及受<大而美法案 >被削減的醫療補助 (Medicaid)在2025~2034年超過
9,000億美元。現ACA進行增強型補貼的恢復提案還在進行中，需總統簽署才能立法恢復，可以是談判交換條件
的一部分；Medicaid刪減是已成法、分階段生效，無法被推翻，只能延緩Medicaid的刪減，其中對工作的要求
限制於今年底生效，早於新國會就任，12/11臨時撥款法案談判是可以納入談判作為局部修正空間的時間點。
• 健護是民主黨國會最直接的受惠產業，健護未來一年本益比仍相對合理：預期若醫療補助刪減延後，醫院將受

惠，ACA補貼若部分恢復，將有利ACA型管理式醫療業者的投保人數止跌回升。以健護未來一年本益比來看，
目前股價仍相對合理。
資料來源：左，中國信託商業銀行整理，2026/11，右，LSEG，2026/11/4。 中國信託商業銀行整理，2026/11/5

分裂國會在兩年任期內的表現優於單一政黨執政

1974年以來標普500指數期中選舉前後的走勢
• 期中選舉前股市較為承壓，但無礙趨勢：根據歷史經驗，期中選舉年通常是總統任期中回撤較深的一年，且選
前的股市波動會明顯放大，然在選後不確定性消退，市場表現往往有所改善，標普500指數在選後的3個月，歷
史中位數報酬率為+6%，而分裂政府(總統和國會分屬不同黨派)的歷史報酬率通常較佳。
• AI相關類股波動高，資金輪動而未明顯離場，美股短線有壓然無礙趨勢：真正驅動市場的因素仍是利率、地緣、
AI，而不是單純從歷史規律推演，近期市場擔憂雲端業者的現金流和債務問題，因此若資料中心的建置時程受
到影響，將進一步影響相關業者的營收和償債時程，提高AI相關類股的波動；另外眾議院是否對川普提出彈劾 ，

也將造成政治上的波動。但就整體趨勢來說，既有政策不會因此出現重大轉折，穩健成長的企業獲利提供重要
支撐，AI類股修正時期資金仍在其他類股間輪動而未明顯離場，美股短線有壓然無礙趨勢。

• 選後反彈高度取決景氣與政策推演：回顧過去美股期中選舉期間，新興市場選前壓抑，選後大多繳出正報酬佳
績，上漲比率超越五成。以參眾兩院控制權皆翻轉的1994年、2006年來看，1944年因美聯準會啟動快速升息，
加上墨西哥金融危機爆發，選後新興市場進入近一年空頭走勢。2006年即便也是分裂政府，全球宏觀環境受惠
貿易繁榮、商品超級周期推動經濟成長，新興市場選後12個月累積報酬亮眼。總體來說，選後不確定性下降，
新興市場可望迎反彈走勢，而即便美國政治版圖翻轉，選後新興市場走勢取決於景氣、政策推演與地緣干擾。
• 市場短線待消化資料中心建設進程對北亞出口影響：美期中選舉對新興市場影響較大面向，主要是關稅與貿易
政策、債務上限，以及對資料中心建設的態度。關稅主導權在白宮推估影響有限 ; 預算與債限談判或對利率造
成波動 ; 短線需消化選後對資料中心監管趨嚴影響北亞出口的失望情緒，然預計長線AI建置仍屬國家重心政策。

則可對政府展開調查，甚至進一步推動彈劾程序。歷史經驗顯示，當民主黨於期中選舉後同時掌控參、眾兩院
時，美元指數平均下跌 4.6%，6次中有4次下跌。整體而言，選舉結果短期內可能因預算協商及債務上限僵局

升高政治風險。2027年美國將再度面臨債務上限問題，美伊地緣政治及貿易戰等變數亦將牽動實質利率走勢。
• 2027年債務上限恐成兩黨鬥爭焦點 ，不利美元：12月11日前，現任國會通過持續撥款決議的可能性偏高 ，但
美國聯邦政府收支預計將於2027年觸及債務上限。受債務上限約束的債務規模預估於2027年3月接近法定上限
500億美元以內 ，之後隨 4月稅收入帳而暫時下降；但若財政赤字增幅高於預期 ，X-Day恐提前到來 。展望
2027年，美國財政議題仍將面臨逆風。共和黨失去完全執政雖有助抑制財政擴張，但政治僵局恐加劇債務上限、
違約及信評遭調降風險，使美元波動加大。

Document 521: タイ中銀、インフレ加速も景気支援を重視して４会合連続で金利据え置き ～原油高やバーツ安など物価上昇リスクがくすぶるなか、_e3429186

Source PDF: all report pdf/タイ中銀、インフレ加速も景気支援を重視して４会合連続で金利据え置き ～原油高やバーツ安など物価上昇リスクがくすぶるなか、_e3429186.pdf
